Replaces the Triton `_hstu_attn_bwd` with a hand-scheduled FlyDSL kernel: **75.06 → 35.35 ms, 261 → 553 TFLOP/s, 2.10×** at the campaign shape (batch 1024, 4 heads, dim 128, bf16, ragged real lengths mean 2700 / max 4095, 2.765 M tokens, `sum(len²)` 7.641e9, target-aware masking off; peak 2307 TFLOP/s verified by a hand-written MFMA loop at 97%+).<br><br>**This is not a fusion win.** Production Triton already computes dq, dk and dv in *one* launch — the sandbox's separate `_hstu_attn_bwd_dq` is dead code and never dispatched. Both kernels run the same five GEMMs in one kv-outer pass. The 2.10× is entirely in how that pass is scheduled and where its bottleneck sits.<br><br>**Triton is register-bound and uses no LDS.** It keeps operands in registers at **498/512 VGPR**, which caps it at 261 TFLOP/s (**11.3%** of peak). FlyDSL stages K/V through **64 KB of LDS** at `BN=128`, fits in **256 arch VGPR + 186 AGPR = 442 with zero spills**, and runs **one wave per SIMD** — 553 TFLOP/s (**24.0%**). Structural levers, each measured: `BN` 64→128 **−17.8%** (46.98 → 38.62 ms); `ds_read_b64_tr_b16`, the gfx950 transpose-on-read, deletes the second transposed LDS copy that was **18.8%** of dk/dv runtime; double-rate `mfma_f32_16x16x32_bf16` (K=32 per issue); and a causal grid reorder with the n-tile slowest-varying, **67.7 → 48.56 ms (−28%)** under one-workgroup-per-CU residency.<br><br>**The dq atomic stream was the actual bottleneck, and it is billed per cache line, not per byte.** Every atomic form on gfx950 saturates at **~2.0e10 64-byte line-requests/s** *regardless of the bytes it carries*, against **8.78e10** for a plain store. Ablation on the fp32-accumulator build isolates it: fused **49.13 ms**, but **35.51 ms** with the atomic swapped for a store at the same address — **atomicity alone was 13.62 ms**. Shrinking bytes therefore does nothing: a bf16 DPP quad-swap pack measured **49.82 ms with `TCC_ATOMIC` unchanged**. What works is halving the *lines*: interleaving `d` in the staged K tile cuts line-requests exactly in half (**0.9997e9 → 0.4999e9**) for **51.45 → 37.29 ms**. Atomicity afterwards is **0.06 ms — fully hidden**. That is what `dq16="bf16p"` buys.<br><br>**Then the schedule was re-swept, because the bottleneck had moved.** `iglp0` had been tuned on the dk/dv-only body, which is now two thirds of the fused one: iglp0 36.98 / iglp1 36.13 / **iglp2 35.04**; shipping iglp2 took **37.31 → 35.39 ms**.<br><br>**Why fused at all:** a separate q-outer dq pass must re-read K and V, **~61 GB per pass** at this shape, which floors *any* two-pass backward near **54 ms** — borne out by the assembly two-pass at 60.09 ms and CK at 48.68 ms, against 35.35 ms fused.<br><br>**In situ the trace agrees, minus a wrapper tax.** Diffing step-52 traces of the two arms (only the FlyDSL one is kept; the Triton side matches the `b48054c` row it supersedes) the kernel goes `_hstu_attn_bwd` **166.36 → `bwd_dkdv_kernel_0` 86.37** `ms/step/rank` (3 calls each, one per HSTU layer) — **1.93×** on the real workload. But the attention-backward *module* only nets **244.15 → 182.19 (−61.95 ms, −25.4%)** and its kernel count rises **122 → 247/step**: production hands q/k/v to the backward as column slices of one `uvqk` buffer, while the kernel's buffer descriptors assume a packed `[N, H, D]` layout, so the wrapper makes q/k/v/dout contiguous going in and copies dq/dk/dv back into the strided views coming out. That is **+116 copy/fill kernels and +15.8 ms/step/rank**, and it is the single largest remaining item — closing it means teaching the kernel the `uvqk` stride, not editing the wrapper.<br><br>**The two measurements corroborate each other:** the trace's −61.95 ms module delta and the −60.93 ms paired wall-clock delta agree to ~1 ms, so the entire e2e gain is the attention backward and nothing else regressed to absorb it. **Not all of it reaches the step, though** — compute-busy drops 533.43 → 468.62 (−64.81 ms) but the step only drops 546.7 → 491.5 (−55.3 ms), because exposed comm (8.41 → 12.77) and idle (4.90 → 10.08) give ~9.5 ms back: removing compute uncovers collectives that were hiding behind it. That is the next thing to chase, not a defect in this kernel.<br><br>**Numerics.** Against the production Triton golden at batch 1024: dq **43.60 dB**, dk **83.49 dB**, dv **bit-identical**. The bf16 dq accumulator is the whole cost — it contributes a 47.43 dB component (**0.425% relative**, ~2.2× the bf16 quant step) and makes dq non-deterministic run-to-run (**50.2 dB**, vs 146.2 dB with `dq16=""`); dk/dv stay exact either way. An fp32 accumulator is one build flag away but hands back the 51.45 → 37.29 ms above.<br><br>**Convergence is unaffected** — the reason the bf16 accumulator is acceptable. Three MLPerf seeds to AUC 0.75 landed at **61.9 / 68.8 / 75.7 M samples** (mean 68.8 M) against the frozen 20-seed Triton RCP sweep's **61.9–80.3 M** (mean 69.3 M): every seed inside the range, the mean **0.20 se** from the sweep mean, on the identical 2.29 M-sample eval grid.<br><br>**Shipped as the default, and it fails loud.** Being the default changes what a fallback means: nobody re-checks a default, so a host that cannot run the kernel (not gfx950, or flydsl not installed) **raises** — naming `HSTU_ATTN_BWD=triton` as the opt-out — instead of quietly handing back the 10.8%. A *shape* the kernel is not specialized for is different: `flydsl_supports()` gates on dim 128, bf16, causal, `max_attn_len == 0`, `contextual_seq_len == 0`, `num_targets ≤ 1`, and anything else still falls back per call, logged once per distinct reason. yambda-5b satisfies all of them. Non-gfx950 launchers (B200) set `HSTU_ATTN_BWD=triton` in the environment, which overrides the gin binding.<br><br>**Confirming it ran:** backend selection is logged once (`[hstu-attn] backward kernel: …`), and `[env bootstrap] HSTU_ATTN_BWD=` records where the value came from. The kernel name in a trace (`bwd_dkdv_kernel_0`) is the independent check — flydsl loads only in the *rank child* processes, never the parent.<br><br>**Tooling: `KernelForge`** (`84b323b`) — the harness behind the ablations, cross-checks and SNR gates quoted above. |
| 2026-08-19 | • TorchRec 3-stage sparse-dist pipeline<br>• input dist deferred behind the<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;forward launch<br>• window iterators chained (warm-keep) | `b1c471a`<br>`93032d9`<br>`ce8a145` | **−9.4 ms/step** (**−1.64%**)<br>14,340 → 14,581 `global_sps` (**+1.68%**)<br>MFU 24.61 → **25.02%**, HFU 10.31 → **10.49%** (**+1.75%**)<br>paired t = 6.03 (19 points), faster at 18/19<br><sub>`START_TS=0`, 200 steps, one A/B pair<br>behind a warmup arm</sub> | **TorchRec 3-stage sparse-dist pipeline.** `$TRAIN_PIPELINE`, **now default 1**. Overlaps batch N's fwd/bwd with batch N+1's embedding input dist and batch N+2's H2D copy on separate streams. Kernel busy time is unchanged — this moves *waiting* off the critical path, not work. Train loss is bit-identical.<br><br>**Stock torchrec barely overlaps.** `progress()` runs `start_sparse_data_dist(N+1)` *before* launching N's forward, so the host pays ~2.5 ms of dedup + splits a2a (then blocks again on the splits sizes) with nothing queued on the compute stream — 5.3 ms dead at the top of every step. `PipelinedForward.__call__` compounds it by enqueueing N's tensor a2a *after* N+1's dedup and then waiting stream-wide, so N's forward waits on N+1's dedup; the FIFO order is already wrong, so a per-batch event cannot fix it.<br><br>**Fix — `$TP_DEFER_INPUT_DIST`** (default 1). `start_sparse_data_dist` stashes `(batch, context)` and `wait_sparse_data_dist` flushes it just before its own work, so the next batch's prep lands *behind* the forward launch and ~130 ms of kernels are queued while the host pays for it. **Step-start idle 5.3 → 1.0 ms**, numerically neutral.<br><br>**Warm-keep — `$TP_WARM_WINDOWS`** (default 1, needs `DOUBLE_BUFFER=1`). Windows used to get a fresh iterator each time, draining and refilling the pipeline at every boundary; at `START_TS=0` the windows are ~10 steps long, so that alone cut the gain to ~0.8%. Chaining the per-window iterators into one continuous stream restores it: in the step-52 trace above, every step carries **exactly one `copy_batch_to_gpu` and one input dist** — no drain step with zero copies, no refill step with three — so a ts=0 trace now reads like an interior-window one.<br><br>**Grad clipping is identical to the sequential path.** `progress()` owns backward and the optimizer step, so an `_optimizer_step` override clips there — same `clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), $GRAD_CLIP_NORM)`, same slot. Sparse tables are untouched in both paths (fused in-backward optimizer).<br><br>**Eval** runs through `detach()`. Stock `attach()` restores the model rewrite but not the input dist, and `fill_pipeline()` short-circuits at `len(batches) >= 2`, so the resumed forward resolved awaitables eval had invalidated — a 0-row embedding tensor into `construct_jagged_tensors`. An `attach` override clears the stale request state and re-issues the input dist for in-flight batches. Against a non-pipelined arm at a 5-step `EVAL_EVERY_DATA_PCT`, all evals match to ~1e-6, mid-window boundaries included.<br><br>**In-window checkpointing** is safe with batches in flight (its guard is gone): `batch_idx_in_window` counts batches *trained*, not batches pulled off the iterator, and the resume skip derives from that same count, so a prefetched-but-untrained batch is re-read, never double-trained. `streaming_resume_test.sh --extra-env TRAIN_PIPELINE=1` passes both scenarios — killed at batch 20, resume re-enters at 20 and reproduces the uninterrupted `window_ne` to logged precision, except step 21 (~0.004, restored metric-accumulator state). |
| 2026-08-18 | • prebuilt window-boundary index<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;(counting sort into `uint32` + offsets) | `a3df514` | **−81.6 s** on a 100-step run (**−38.8%**)<br>210.2 → 128.6 s wall clock<br>3,897 → 6,370 effective `global_sps`<br>no steady-state MFU/HFU delta — this row buys<br>back boundary-crossing stalls, not compute, and<br>its numbers are wall clock over a short run<br>rather than a matched per-step comparison | **Prebuilt window-boundary index.** Every boundary masked the whole 17.1 GiB anchor-timestamp array — ~2.7 s per boundary, per rank, to recover a small slice of it. A counting sort now buckets anchors by window into a `uint32` array plus offsets, so a boundary is a slice. `uint32` (2.29 B anchors, under the 4.29 B ceiling) keeps it to 8.5 GB; the earlier argsort variant was rejected at ~52 GB, which evicted the event-array page cache into NCCL watchdog timeouts. Built once per (dataset, window_seconds) in 53.7 s, persisted beside `anchor_ts_*.npy` and mmap'd read-only by all ranks. `$DL_WINDOW_INDEX`, **default 1**. The win scales with boundaries crossed, so it is largest at `START_TS=0`; steady state is untouched. |
| 2026-08-18 | • pinned (page-locked) dataloader<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;host buffers | `c7a5a05`<br>`33ed942` (trace + log row) | **−47.6 ms/step** (**−8.1%**)<br>13,930 → 15,155 `global_sps`<br>HFU 9.67 → **10.52%** (**+8.79%**, = the throughput<br>gain; a host-side stall fix, not a compute one)<br><sub>re-measured on top of the window index. HFU<br>reconstructed from this row's `global_sps` and the<br>campaign's logged `fill` of 40.45%, an identity<br>that reproduces the logged HFU of every arm in<br>these campaigns to within 0.02 pp.</sub> | **Pinned dataloader host buffers.** Page-locked memory can be DMA'd straight to the GPU; the loaders were handing over pageable batches, which the driver stages through a bounce buffer while the training thread blocks (~2.4 GB/s on the ~215 MB/step H2D copy, ~89 ms/step of stall). `$DL_PIN_MEMORY`, **default 1**. Costs a bounded amount of unswappable host RAM. |

---

## Misc

### LN-dropout separated-RNG path on MI350 (pre-MLPerf-proposal)

`_ln_mul_dropout` had a legacy variant (one program per row, RNG fused inline)
and a separated-RNG variant (multiple rows per program, dropout mask computed
once and reused by the backward) that was gated to Blackwell. gfx950 benefits
from the same structure, so `use_separated_rng_ln_mul_dropout()` enables it on
MI350 too (`b5e8e2a`,
which also pins the fast `nonkdim:16` attention autotune configs). It was worth
+5.6% when measured, but that was before the MLPerf reference implementation
settled, against different defaults, so the number is not comparable to anything
in the tables above and it is not carried in the optimization log.

### A 200-step harness under-reads anything that scales with sequence length

The throughput config in these tables is `START_TS=0 NUM_TRAIN_TS=31` with
`NUM_TRAIN_BATCHES=200`, which at GBS 8192 never leaves the **first 30 data
windows**. Those are the *shortest-history* windows in the dataset: `fill` (mean
sequence length over the 4096 padded budget) is **41.9%** there and rises to
**47.4%** by step 9000, because a streaming run accumulates user history as it
walks the timeline. A convergence run spends almost all of its steps past that
band.

So any optimization whose saving scales with tokens per batch is measured at the
bottom of its own range. Both 2026-08-25 rows are that kind: the dedup expand's
backward is one launch over `indices = tokens`, and attention is quadratic in
length. Measured as one paired 3-seed A/B (same nodes, same tree, only the two
knobs flipped), the step gain is:

| band | `fill` | speedup |
|---|---|---|
| steps 20–200 | 41.9% | **1.087×** |
| steps 205–1000 | 44.2% | 1.120× |
| steps 1005–2000 | 45.1% | 1.125× |
| steps 2005–4000 | 46.2% | 1.126× |
| steps 4005–6000 | 47.1% | **1.130×** |

The 200-step figure is not wrong — it reproduces the two rows' own prediction
(1.081×) to half a point, at the `fill` those rows were traced at. It is
answering a narrower question than "what does a run cost". **Read a 200-step
throughput delta as a lower bound for any length-scaling change, and settle the
magnitude on a convergence pair.** The reverse trap is in the
[LN-dropout note](#ln-dropout-separated-rng-path-on-mi350-pre-mlperf-proposal)
and in the FlyDSL forward row: a ratio measured on the wrong length pool can be
inflated just as easily.

Wall clock moves less than the step does, and that is arithmetic rather than a
disagreement: eval is **~8.5%** of a convergence run's wall clock and is
untouched by either knob (its passes moved +0.4 / −0.6 / +0.3%), so a 1.130×
step gain arrives as **1.114×** to the AUC-0.75 threshold. Quote whichever
answers the question, but do not expect them to be the same number.

**Confirmed again, 2026-09-16, on a different stack.** The three-arm ladder in
the 2026-09-16 four-rung ladder (job **37996**) measured **+13.08%** at 200
steps. Paired on identical step indices across the same 3 convergence seeds
(415/454/376 paired steps), the same arms sustain **+18.5%**:

| band | `fill` | speedup | n |
|---|---|---|---|
| steps 20–200 | 41.9% | **1.114×** | 30 |
| steps 205–1000 | 44.0% | 1.128× | 120 |
| steps 1005–2000 | 45.5% | 1.176× | 150 |
| steps 2005–4000 | 46.3% | 1.181× | 300 |
| steps 4005–6000 | 47.1% | 1.185× | 300 |
| steps 6005–9240 | 47.5% | **1.190×** | 345 |

Monotone in `fill` again, and the gap is larger than 2026-08-25's — **5.4 pp**
between the harness figure and the sustained one, against 4.3 pp. Two of the
three arms scale with tokens per batch (the `ec_bf16_output` cast and the
segment-reduce expand backward both run over the **expanded** token count), so
this is the same mechanism, not a coincidence. Wall clock again lands below the
step gain: **1.146×** to AUC 0.75.

### `NUM_EVAL_BATCHES=0` does not mean "no eval"

It removes the per-pass **cap**, so each eval pass runs the *full* holdout
instead of a truncated one. What decides *whether* eval runs is the cadence
pair, and `EVAL_EVERY_DATA_PCT` **defaults to 0.001** — eval every 0.1% of the
data. Disabling eval takes **both** knobs:

```bash
EVAL_EVERY_DATA_PCT=0 EVAL_EVERY_N_WINDOWS=0    # train-only
```

Setting only `EVAL_EVERY_N_WINDOWS=0` changes nothing: 0 is already its default
and means "defer to the data-fraction cadence", which is on.

**As a throughput mistake this is merely slow, but as a *tracing* mistake it is
silent and total.** The streaming loop's profiler counter advances across train
**and** eval batches, so `Profiler.trace_steps = [52]` stops meaning "training
step 52" the moment eval runs — and at `START_TS=0 NUM_TRAIN_TS=31` the interval
is computed over a small anchor count, so eval fires early and often. A traced
pair collected this way had `ProfilerStep#54` sitting on **eval batch 11**. Eval
runs a **forward-only** pipeline (see the 2026-08-24 row), so those traces
contained no backward pass, no optimizer step and no autograd thread at all —
half the GPU work, and precisely the half most kernel work is in. Nothing in the
run log says so; the trace simply looks small.

**Three checks that catch it, in increasing order of effort.** The trace has
**two CPU threads** in a healthy capture — the main python thread and the
autograd thread — and **one** if the profiled steps were eval batches. Its root
ops under a `ProfilerStep` read `## forward N ## / ## backward N ## /
Optimizer.step`, and the `N` there **equals the ProfilerStep number**; if the
batch index has drifted below the step number, eval has been consuming steps.
And `grep -c "eval - Step" $LOG` should be 0 for a train-only run (the
end-of-run eval always runs, so a handful of lines at the very end is expected).

Widening the profiler window does not help and will look like it might: at
`Profiler.active = 24` the trace grows to 45 MB per rank, captures 24 steps and
4.1 s of GPU time, and still contains **zero** backward ops. That is the tell
that the window is landing in the wrong *phase* rather than being too short.

### The first captured step is a profiler artifact; the last one is not

A 5-step Kineto window yields **3** usable steps. Average all 5 and every
figure you derive is wrong by a few percent, in a direction that changes
depending on what you are comparing against. But the two ends are wrong for
*different reasons*, and only the first is the profiler's fault.

Measured on job **36093** (`c06-08`), and reproduced on job **35310**
(`c23-08`, different host, different tree) — so this is a property of the
capture, not of a node or an arm:

| capture | first step (52) | steps 53-55 | step 56 | first step is |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `tr_all3` 36093 | 361.73 | 295.50 | 251.64 | **+66.2 ms** |
| `tr_ctl` 36093 | 364.01 | 309.46 | 273.54 | **+54.6 ms** |
| `all3` 35310 | 363.79 | 316.41 | 272.44 | **+47.4 ms** |

The right-hand column is labelled **step 56**, not "last step", and that
distinction is the whole point of the correction below. All three captures ran
`Profiler.active = 5` with the window at 52, so their last recorded step *was*
step 56 — window position and absolute step number were perfectly confounded.

**The first step is waiting, not work.** In `tr_all3` step 52 the collective
*count* is unchanged (12 collectives, same as every other step) but their
duration inflates from 24 to 97 ms, exposed collective time goes **8.7 → 57.9**
ms/rank/step, and GPU idle goes **8.0 → 47.1**. Compute *falls* (256.7 vs
270-283), which is the signature of a stall rather than extra work. The cause
is visible in the step-entry timestamps: ranks enter step 52 up to **24.4 ms**
apart, against ≤2.4 ms on every later step. Kineto's warmup→active transition
is per-process and unsynchronized, so the ranks that arm the profiler slowly
enter late and every other rank blocks in the next collective.

**The first step does not reach the profiler-off arms.** In `ctl`/`all3` the
bucket covering the window (step 60) reads **318.7** / **317.6** ms,
indistinguishable from its neighbours — though see the caveat below on what a
bucket mean can and cannot resolve.

**CORRECTION — the tail is two separate effects, and the deflation is real
work, not a flush.** This section previously read "the last step is the flush"
and attributed the ~13% dip in the right-hand column to profiler teardown. That
was wrong, from the confound noted above. Job **36446** (`active = 7`) and
probe job **36454** (window moved to `[45, 55]`) separate them:

- **Teardown skew is real and is the profiler.** The tail steps go ragged as
  the eight ranks finish flushing at different times. Cross-rank spread at step
  56: **25.06 ms** at `active = 6` (36227) → **0.66 ms** at `active = 7`
  (36446), against ~1 ms on clean steps. This is what `Profiler.active = 7` +
  `keep_active = 5` fixes — record 52-58, export 52-56, and the ragged steps
  never reach the artifact.
- **The deflation is not the profiler.** Step 56 runs ~13% short regardless of
  where the window sits. Probe 36454 put it at window offset **+1** instead of
  **+4** and it came back at **280.62 ms** — 0.2 ms from its value under the
  other placement (280.82) — while offset +4 in that probe (step 59) was
  **388.46 ms**, not deflated at all. The dip follows the **absolute step
  number**. It is a property of the streaming loop, present with the profiler
  off; `METRIC_LOG_FREQ = 10` makes `step_ms` a 10-step bucket mean, which
  cannot resolve a single short step. Steps ≡ 8, 9 mod 10 are correspondingly
  elevated (448.09 / 388.46 in the probe).

Consequence for anyone reading a capture: **no window placement avoids step
56**, and trimming cannot fix it. Read **52-55** for per-step wall time. Step 56
ships in the artifact because it is genuine recorded behaviour, but it is not a
clean step and picking it as "the fastest step" picks the one that is lying.

#### Corollary: the "profiler tax" is one bucket, and a traced arm is a valid rate arm

The often-quoted **+14.9%** profiler tax (`tr_ctl` 358.74 vs `ctl` 312.25 over
19 points) is not a per-step cost. It is confined to the one `METRIC_LOG_FREQ=10`
bucket that contains the capture and the ~19.5 MB/rank trace serialization.

Mind the units: `step_ms` is a **bucket mean**, not a single step — it tracks
`d(elapsed_sec)/10` exactly. That bucket takes **8.89 s** (`tr_ctl`) and
**8.81 s** (`tr_all3`) of wall clock against ~3.1 s and ~3.0 s for their
neighbours, i.e. **+891** and **+881** ms *per step averaged over ten*, which
is how step 60 comes to read **1202.9** / **1185.6** ms against ~312 / ~300
either side. The profiler-off arms show **-0.02 s** and **-0.01 s** on the same
bucket. Drop that one point:

| arm | mean, 19 pts | mean, excl. step 60 | profiler-off counterpart |
|---|---|---|---|
| `tr_ctl` | 358.74 | **311.85** | `ctl` 311.90 (**−0.02%**) |
| `tr_all3` | 345.97 | **299.32** | `all3` 299.30 (**+0.01%**) |

Outside the capture bucket the profiler costs nothing measurable, and the
traced arms reproduce the profiler-off A/B. Compare like with like — the
published **−3.84%** is over all 19 points, and the traced **−4.02%** is over
18, since the traced arms must drop the capture bucket. Drop step 60 from the
profiler-off arms too (it is a legitimate step there, so this is only for
window-matching) and they read **311.90 → 299.30 = −4.04%**, against the traced
**−4.02%**: **0.02 pp** apart.

So a trace campaign does **not** need separate rate arms. `warmup → tr_ctl →
tr_all3` yields both the trace and the rate in ~15 minutes instead of ~33,
provided the aggregator drops the capture bucket. Report the rate from the
other 18 points and the trace from capture steps 53-55.

### The first arm of a paired run pays a ~23 s cold-page-cache tax

`STAGE_DATA=1` puts the 372 GB dataset on node-local NVMe, but the **page cache
is still cold**. The first arm to run faults window 1 in from NVMe; the second
arm finds it resident. Measured on three nodes in job **36508**, first-batch
`data-wait` at the opening window:

| seed | 1st arm | 2nd arm | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 75.6 s | 52.0 s | 23.6 s |
| 2 | 78.4 s | 54.6 s | 23.8 s |
| 3 | 75.6 s | 53.5 s | 22.1 s |

It is **not** node contention: 75.6 s reproduces to the decimal on two different
hosts, it is independent of which arm runs first, and steady-state waits after
window 1 are **~150 ms**. It is a one-off first-touch cost.

**It biases a same-node paired A/B by ~0.65%** of a ~58 min run, always against
whichever arm runs first — against an effect size of a few percent. Job 36508
shows it directly, because its arm order was counterbalanced: with `ctl` first
the wall ratio read **0.918x** and **0.972x**; with `a2` first it read
**1.000x**, the penalty landing on the other arm and cancelling the gain.
Samples-to-threshold were **identical (delta 0)** in all three pairs, so this is
purely a wall-clock artifact.

**This affects the recorded convergence campaign too.** Jobs 35508/35509/35510
ran `ctl` first in all three seeds with no warmup arm, so their **0.961x** carries
the same bias in the same direction; the unbiased figure is nearer **0.967x**.

**Fix: run a short warmup arm before the measured pair**, exactly as the
throughput ladders do. A natural experiment separates what warmup does and does
not fix - job 36479 had a warmup arm, job 36508 did not:

| stall source | with warmup (36479) | without (36508) | warmup fixes? |
|---|---|---|---|
| cold page cache, window 1 | **0 stalls** in all 5 rate arms | 23 s first-arm tax | **yes** |
| profiler capture bucket | 2 stalls, max 1607 ms, `tr_*` only | n/a | **no** - drop the bucket |
| node contention burst | none on `c06-08` | 29 on `c15-18` | **no** - node-local |

The profiler stalls survive warmup because they are not data waits: `tr_ctl` and
`tr_a2` ran in the same allocation, after the same warmup, on the same warm node,
and still showed them. See [the profiler-artifact
section](#the-first-captured-step-is-a-profiler-artifact-the-last-one-is-not).

Second-best remedies if a warmup arm is not affordable: drop window 1 from the
wall measurement, or subtract the logged first-batch `data-wait` (it is printed
per window as `[boundary] train_ts=N train first-batch data-wait=...ms`).

### GPU clock lock can mask all perf changes

A node-level GPU clock lock will silently invalidate any benchmark on this
machine, so check it before trusting numbers.

During this work all 8 GPUs were stuck in **`perf_determinism`** performance
level at **sclk 1093 MHz** (DPM level 1) while the real max is **2200 MHz**
(level 2) — despite 100% utilization, ~370 W of power headroom (629 / 1000 W),
and low temps (~50 °C). This was **not** thermal/power throttling; it was
leftover node state from a prior job.

Effect: a **uniform ~1.87× slowdown of every Triton compute kernel**
(`2200 / 1093 ≈ 2.0×`), including kernels unrelated to any code change. It made
the LN-dropout change look like a regression until the clock state was found.

**It recurred on 2026-08-21 on `e12-18`, and the auto-restore did not catch it.**
Every arm of the first e2e round for the two FlyDSL optimizations above logged
`Performance Level: perf_determinism` on all 8 GPUs — the launch script's
`perf-level check` printed the lock and the run proceeded anyway, because inside
the container it lacks permission to reset it. Those arms ran ~15–25% slow
(565–623 `ms/step` vs 474 restored) and the paired throughput deltas scattered
from −1.4% to +7.6% across identical pairs. `rocm-smi --setperflevel auto` from
an `srun` on the node — *outside* the container — did work.

Two practical consequences: **assert the perf level before every arm, not once
per campaign** (a lock can appear mid-campaign, and a benchmark harness that
checks once will happily attribute it to the change under test), and **treat the
`perf-level check` line in the launch log as data to grep after the fact**, since
it is the only record that a given arm was clean.

#### Detect + fix

```bash
rocm-smi --showperflevel          # expect "auto", not perf_determinism/manual/low
rocm-smi -d 0 --showclocks        # expect sclk ~2000+ MHz under load
rocm-smi --setperflevel auto      # restore boost
```

**On 2026-09-01 neither of the two documented escape hatches worked**, and this
matters because a lock that cannot be cleared silently costs ~9%. On `c17-18`,
`rocm-smi --setperflevel auto` from an `srun` on the node — the fix the note
above says did work in August — failed with `sudo: a password is required`, and
writing `/sys/class/drm/card*/device/power_dpm_force_performance_level` directly
as `$USER` was equally refused. What did work was going in through the
**privileged training container**, which the job has already started:

```bash
srun --jobid=$JID --overlap -N1 -n1 bash -c 'docker exec -u 0 yambda_primus bash -c "
  for f in /sys/class/drm/card*/device/power_dpm_force_performance_level; do
    echo auto > \$f
  done
  rocm-smi --showperflevel"'
```

That has a scheduling consequence worth planning for: the clocks can only be
fixed **after** the job holds the node and its container is up, so a benchmark
driver has to wait for `RUNNING`, clear the lock, and only then let training
reach its first timed step — rather than asserting once before submitting. The
driver behind the 2026-09-01 log row does exactly that, per arm.

`scripts/launch_slurm.sh` (worker phase) now logs the perf level + a live `sclk` sample on
every launch, auto-restores `auto` if it finds a `perf_determinism`/`manual`/`low`
lock, and warns (to reset from the host) if it lacks permission inside the
container. **Always sanity-check `sclk ≈ 2000+ MHz` before trusting a benchmark.**
